[[Love]]’s heralds should be thoughts,<br>Which ten times faster glides than the sun’s beams<br>Driving back shadows over louring hills.<br>Therefore do nimble-pinioned doves draw [[Love]],<br>And therefore hath the [[Wind\|wind]]-swift Cupid wings.<br>Now is the sun upon the highmost [[Hill\|hill]]<br>Of this day’s [[Journey\|journey]], and from nine till twelve<br>Is three long hours, yet she is not come.<br>Had she affections and warm youthful [[Blood\|blood]]<br>She would be as swift in motion as a ball.<br>My words would bandy her to my sweet [[Love\|love]],<br>And his to me.<br>But old folks, many feign as they were dead—<br>Unwieldy, slow, heavy, and pale as lead. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Nurse]] and Peter |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | O God, she comes! O honey [[Nurse]], what news?<br>Hast thou met with him ? Send thy man away. |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Peter, stay at the gate. Exit Peter |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Now, good sweet [[Nurse]]—O Lord, why look‘st thou sad ?<br>Though news be sad, yet tell them merrily;<br>If good, thou sham’st the music of sweet news<br>By playing it to me with so sour a face. |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | I am a-weary. Give me leave a while.<br>Fie, how my bones ache. What a jaunce have I! |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | I would thou hadst my bones and I thy news.<br>Nay, come, I pray thee speak, good, good [[Nurse]], speak. |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Jesu, what haste! Can you not stay a while?<br>Do you not see that I am out of breath? |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | How art thou out of breath when thou hast breath<br>To say to me that thou art out of breath?<br>The excuse that thou dost make in this delay<br>Is longer than the tale thou dost excuse.<br>Is thy news good or bad? Answer to that.<br>Say either, and I’ll stay the circumstance.<br>Let me be satisfied: is’t good or bad? |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | [[Well]], you have made a simple choice. You know not how to choose a man. [[Romeo]]? No, not he; though his face be better than any man‘s, yet his leg excels all men’s, and for a hand and a foot and a [[Body\|body]], though they be not to be talked on, yet they are past compare. He is not the flower of courtesy, but, I’ll warrant him, as gentle as a [[Lamb\|lamb]]. Go thy ways, wench. Serve God. What, have you dined at [[Home\|home]]? |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | No, no. But all this did I know before.<br>What says he of our [[Marriage\|marriage]]—what of that? |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Lord, how my head aches! What a head have I!<br>It beats as it would fall in twenty pieces.<br>My back— |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Juliet]] rubs her back |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | a’ t’other side—ah, my back, my back!<br>Beshrew your heart for sending me about<br>To catch my [[Death\|death]] with jauncing up and down. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | I’[[Faith\|faith]], I am sorry that thou art not [[Well\|well]].<br>Sweet, sweet, sweet [[Nurse]], tell me, what says my [[Love\|love]]? |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Your [[Love\|love]] says, like an honest gentleman, and a<br>courteous, and a kind, and a handsome, and, I warrant,<br>a virtuous—where is your mother? |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Where is my mother? Why, she is within.<br>Where should she be? How oddly thou repliest!<br>‘Your [[Love\|love]] says like an honest gentleman<br>“Where is your mother?” ’ |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | O, God’s Lady dear!<br>Are you so hot? Marry come up, I trow.<br>Is this the poultice for my aching bones?<br>Henceforward do your messages yourself. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Here’s such a coil ! Come, what says [[Romeo]]? |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Have you got leave to go to shrift today? |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | I have. |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Then hie you hence to [[Friar Laurence]]’ cell.<br>There stays a husband to make you a wife.<br>Now comes the wanton [[Blood\|blood]] up in your cheeks.<br>They’ll be in scarlet straight at any news.<br>Hie you to church. I must another way,<br>To fetch a ladder by the which your [[Love\|love]]<br>Must climb a bird’s nest soon, when it is dark.<br>I am the drudge, and toil in your delight,<br>But you shall bear the burden soon at [[Night\|night]].<br>Go, I’ll to dinner. Hie you to the cell. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Hie to high fortune! Honest [[Nurse]], farewell. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally |

### Atto 2, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Friar Laurence]] and [[Romeo]] |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | So smile the heavens upon this holy act<br>That after-hours with sorrow chide us not! |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | Amen, amen. But come what sorrow can,<br>It cannot countervail the exchange of joy<br>That one short minute gives me in her sight.<br>Do thou but close our hands with holy words,<br>Then [[Love\|love]]-devouring [[Death\|death]] do what he dare—<br>It is enough I may but call her mine. |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | These violent delights have violent ends,<br>And in their triumph die like [[Fire\|fire]] and powder,<br>Which as they kiss consume. The sweetest honey<br>Is loathsome in his own deliciousness,<br>And in the taste confounds the appetite.<br>Therefore [[Love\|love]] moderately. Long [[Love\|love]] doth so.<br>Too swift arrives as tardy as too slow. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Juliet]] Somewhat fast, and embraceth [[Romeo]] |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | Here comes the lady. O, so light a foot<br>Will ne’er wear out the everlasting flint.<br>A [[Lover\|lover]] may bestride the gossamers<br>That idles in the wanton [[Summer\|summer]] air,<br>And yet not fall, so light is [[Vanity\|vanity]]. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Good even to my ghostly confessor. |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | [[Romeo]] shall thank thee, daughter, for us both. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | As much to him, else is his thanks too much. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | Ah, [[Juliet]], if the measure of thy joy<br>Be heaped like mine, and that thy skill be more<br>To blazon it, then sweeten with thy breath<br>This neighbour air, and let rich music’s tongue<br>Unfold the imagined happiness that both<br>Receive in either by this dear encounter. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Conceit, more rich in matter than in words,<br>Brags of his substance, not of ornament.<br>They are but beggars that can count their worth,<br>But my true [[Love\|love]] is grown to such excess<br>I cannot sum up some of half my wealth. |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | Come, come with me, and we will make short work,<br>For, by your [[Leaves\|leaves]], you shall not stay [[Alone\|alone]]<br>Till Holy Church incorporate two in one. Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Mercutio]] with his page, [[Benvolio]], and men |
| [[Benvolio\|BENVOLIO]] | I pray thee, good [[Mercutio]], let’s retire.<br>The day is hot, the Capels are abroad,<br>And if we meet we shall not scape a brawl,<br>For now, these hot days, is the mad [[Blood\|blood]] stirring. |
| [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] | Thou art like one of these fellows that, when he enters the confines of a tavern, claps me his [[Sword\|sword]] upon the table and says ‘God send me no need of thee’, and by the operation of the second cup, draws him on the drawer when indeed there is no need. |
| [[Benvolio\|BENVOLIO]] | Am I like such a fellow? |
| [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] | Come, come, thou art as hot a jack in thy mood as any in [[Italy]], and as soon moved to be moody, and as soon moody to be moved. |
| [[Benvolio\|BENVOLIO]] | And what to? |
| [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] | Nay, an there were two such, we should have none shortly, for one would kill the other. Thou—why, thou wilt quarrel with a man that hath a hair more or a hair less in his beard than thou hast. Thou wilt quarrel with a man for cracking nuts, having no other reason but because thou hast hazel eyes. What eye but such an eye would spy out such a quarrel? Thy head is as full of quarrels as an egg is full of meat, and yet thy head hath been beaten as addle as an egg for quarrelling. Thou hast quarrelled with a man for coughing in the street because he hath wakened thy dog that hath lain asleep in the sun. Didst thou not fall out with a tailor for wearing his new doublet before Easter; with another for tying his new shoes with old ribbon? And yet thou wilt tutor me from quarrelling! |
| [[Benvolio\|BENVOLIO]] | An I were so apt to quarrel as thou art, any man should buy the fee-simple of my life for an hour and a quarter. |
| [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] | The fee simple? O, simple! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Tybalt]], Petruccio, and others |
| [[Benvolio\|BENVOLIO]] | By my head, here comes the Capulets. |
| [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] | By my heel, I care not. |
| [[Tybalt\|TYBALT]] | (to Petruccio and the others)<br>Follow me close, for I will speak to them.<br>(To the Montagues) Gentlemen, good e’en. A word with<br>one of you. |
| [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] | And but one word with one of us? Couple it with something: make it a word and a blow. |
| [[Tybalt\|TYBALT]] | You shall find me apt enough to that, sir, an you will give me occasion. |
| [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] | Could you not take some occasion without giving ? |
| [[Tybalt\|TYBALT]] | [[Mercutio]], thou consort’st with [[Romeo]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] ‘Consort’ ? What, dost thou make us minstrels? An thou make minstrels of us, look to hear nothing but discords. touching his rapier Here’s my fiddlestick; here’s that shall make you [[Dance\|dance]]. Zounds—‘Consort’! |
| [[Benvolio\|BENVOLIO]] | We talk here in the public haunt of men.<br>Either withdraw unto some private place,<br>Or reason coldly of your grievances,<br>Or else depart. Here all eyes gaze on us. |
| [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] | Men’s eyes were made to look, and let them gaze.<br>I will not budge for no man’s pleasure, I. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Romeo]] |
| [[Tybalt\|TYBALT]] | [[Well]], peace be with you, sir. Here comes my man. |
| [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] | But I’ll be hanged, sir, if he wear your livery.<br>Marry, go before to field, he’ll be your follower.<br>Your worship in that sense may call him ‘man’. |
| [[Tybalt\|TYBALT]] | [[Romeo]], the [[Love\|love]] I bear thee can afford<br>No better term than this: thou art a [[Villain\|villain]]. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | [[Tybalt]], the reason that I have to [[Love\|love]] thee<br>Doth much excuse the appertaining rage<br>To such a greeting. [[Villain]] am I none.<br>Therefore, farewell. I see thou knowest me not. |
| [[Tybalt\|TYBALT]] | Boy, this shall not excuse the injuries<br>That thou hast done me. Therefore turn and draw. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | I do protest I never injured thee,<br>But [[Love\|love]] thee better than thou canst devise<br>Till thou shalt know the reason of my [[Love\|love]].<br>And so, good [[Capulet]]—which name I tender<br>As dearly as mine own—be satisfied. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] drawing |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | O calm, dishonourable, vile submission! 1<br>Alla stoccado carries it away.<br>[[Tybalt]], you ratcatcher, come, will you walk? |
| [[Tybalt\|TYBALT]] | What wouldst thou have with me? |
| [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] | Good [[King]] of Cats, nothing but one of your nine lives. That I mean to make bold withal, and, as you shall use me hereafter, dry-beat the rest of the eight. Will you pluck your [[Sword\|sword]] out of his pilcher by the ears? Make haste, lest mine be about your ears ere it be out. |
| [[Tybalt\|TYBALT]] | (drawing) I am for you. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | Gentle [[Mercutio]], put thy rapier up. |
| [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] | (to [[Tybalt]]) Come, sir, your passado.<br>They fight |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] drawing |
| [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] | Draw, [[Benvolio]]. Beat down their weapons.<br>Gentlemen, for shame forbear this outrage.<br>[[Tybalt]], [[Mercutio]], the Prince expressly hath<br>Forbid this bandying in [[Verona]] streets.<br>Hold, [[Tybalt]], good [[Mercutio]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Romeo]] beats down their points and rushes between them. [[Tybalt]] under [[Romeo]]’s arm thrusts [[Mercutio]] in |
| *(didascalia)* | PETRUCCIO Away, [[Tybalt]]! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Tybalt]], Petruccio, and their followers |
| [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] | I am hurt.<br>A plague o’ both your houses. I am sped.<br>Is he gone, and hath nothing? |
| [[Benvolio\|BENVOLIO]] | What, art thou hurt? |
| [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] | Ay, ay, a scratch, a scratch; marry, ’tis enough.<br>Where is my page? Go, [[Villain\|villain]]. Fetch a surgeon. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit page |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | [[Courage]], man. The hurt cannot be much. |
| [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] | No, ‘tis not so deep as a [[Well\|well]], nor so wide as a church door, but ’tis enough. ’Twill serve. Ask for me tomorrow, and you shall find me a [[Grave\|grave]] man. I am peppered, I warrant, for this world. A plague o’ both your houses! Zounds, a dog, a rat, a mouse, a cat, to scratch a man to [[Death\|death]]! A braggart, a rogue, a [[Villain\|villain]], that fights by the book of arithmetic! Why the devil came you between us ? I was hurt under your arm. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | I thought all for the best. |
| [[Mercutio\|MERCUTIO]] | Help me into some [[house]], [[Benvolio]],<br>Or I shall faint. A plague o’ both your houses.<br>They have made worms’ meat of me.<br>I have it, and soundly, too. Your houses! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but [[Romeo]] |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | This gentleman, the Prince’s near ally,<br>My very friend, hath got this mortal hurt<br>In my behalf, my reputation stained<br>With [[Tybalt]]’s slander—[[Tybalt]], that an hour<br>Hath been my cousin! O sweet [[Juliet]],<br>Thy [[Beauty\|beauty]] hath made me effeminate,<br>And in my temper softened valour’s steel. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Benvolio]] |
| [[Benvolio\|BENVOLIO]] | O [[Romeo]], [[Romeo]], brave [[Mercutio]] is dead!<br>That gallant spirit hath aspired the clouds,<br>Which too untimely here did scorn the earth. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | This day’s black fate on more days doth depend.<br>This but begins the woe others must end. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Tybalt]] |
| [[Benvolio\|BENVOLIO]] | Here comes the furious [[Tybalt]] back again. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | He gad in triumph, and [[Mercutio]] slain ?<br>Away to heaven, respective lenity,<br>And [[Fire\|fire]]-eyed fury be my conduct now.<br>Now, [[Tybalt]], take the ‘[[Villain\|villain]]’ back again<br>That late thou gav’st me, for [[Mercutio]]’s [[Soul\|soul]]<br>Is but a little way above our heads,<br>Staying for thine to keep him company.<br>Either thou, or I, or both must go with him. |
| [[Tybalt\|TYBALT]] | Thou, wretched boy, that didst consort him here,<br>Shalt with him hence. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | This shall determine that.<br>They fight. [[Tybalt]] is wounded. He falls and dies |
| [[Benvolio\|BENVOLIO]] | [[Romeo]], away, be gone.<br>The citizens are up, and [[Tybalt]] slain.<br>Stand not amazed. The Prince will doom thee [[Death\|death]]<br>If thou art taken. Hence, be gone, away. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | O, I am fortune’s [[Fool\|fool]]! |
| [[Benvolio\|BENVOLIO]] | Why dost thou stay? |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[Romeo]] |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Citizens of the watchi |
| *(didascalia)* | CITIZEN 0F THE WATCH |
| [[Benvolio\|BENVOLIO]] | Which way ran he that killed [[Mercutio]]?<br>[[Tybalt]], that murderer, which way ran he? |
| [[Benvolio\|BENVOLIO]] | There lies that [[Tybalt]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | CITIZEN OF THE WATCH (to [[Tybalt]]) Up, sir, go with me. |
| [[Benvolio\|BENVOLIO]] | I charge thee in the Prince’s name, obey. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Prince, old Montague, [[Capulet]], their Wives, and all |
| PRINCE | Where are the vile beginners of this fray? |
| [[Benvolio\|BENVOLIO]] | O noble Prince, I can discover all<br>The unlucky manage of this fatal brawl.<br>There lies the man, slain by young [[Romeo]],<br>That slew thy kinsman, brave [[Mercutio]]. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | [[Tybalt]], my cousin, O, my brother’s [[Child\|child]]!<br>O Prince, O cousin, husband! O, the [[Blood\|blood]] is spilled<br>Of my dear kinsman ! Prince, as thou art true,<br>For [[Blood\|blood]] of ours shed [[Blood\|blood]] of Montague!<br>O cousin, cousin! |
| PRINCE | [[Benvolio]], who began this fray? |
| [[Benvolio\|BENVOLIO]] | [[Tybalt]], here slain, whom [[Romeo]]’s hand did slay.<br>[[Romeo]], that spoke him fair, bid him bethink<br>How nice the quarrel was, and urged withal<br>Your high displeasure. All this-utterèd<br>With gentle breath, calm look, knees humbly bowed—<br>Could not take truce with the unruly spleen<br>Of [[Tybalt]] deaf to peace, but that he tilts<br>With piercing steel at bold [[Mercutio]]’s breast,<br>Who, all as hot, turns deadly point to point,<br>And, with a martial scorn, with one hand beats<br>Cold [[Death\|death]] aside, and with the other sends<br>It back to [[Tybalt]], whose dexterity<br>Retorts it. [[Romeo]], he cries aloud,<br>‘Hold, friends, friends, part!’ and swifter than his<br>tongue<br>His agent arm beats down their fatal points,<br>And ’twixt them rushes, underneath whose arm<br>An envious thrust from [[Tybalt]] hit the life<br>Of stout [[Mercutio]], and then [[Tybalt]] fled,<br>But by and by comes back to [[Romeo]],<br>Who had but newly entertained [[Revenge\|revenge]],<br>And to’t they go like [[Lightning\|lightning]]; for ere I<br>Could draw to part them was stout [[Tybalt]] slain,<br>And as he fell did [[Romeo]] turn and fly.<br>This is the [[Truth\|truth]], or let [[Benvolio]] die. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | He is a kinsman to the Montague.<br>Affection makes him false; he speaks not true.<br>Some twenty of them fought in this black strife,<br>And all those twenty could but kill one life.<br>I beg for justice, which thou, Prince, must give.<br>[[Romeo]] slew [[Tybalt]]; [[Romeo]] must not live. |
| PRINCE | [[Romeo]] slew him, he slew [[Mercutio]].<br>Who now the price of his dear [[Blood\|blood]] doth owe? |
| *(didascalia)* | MONTAGUE |
| PRINCE | Not [[Romeo]], Prince. He was [[Mercutio]]’s friend.<br>His fault concludes but what the law should end,<br>The life of [[Tybalt]]. |
| PRINCE | And for that offence<br>Immediately we do [[Exile\|exile]] him hence.<br>I have an interest in your hate’s proceeding;<br>My [[Blood\|blood]] for your rude brawls doth lie a-bleeding.<br>But I’ll amerce you with so strong a fine<br>That you shall all repent the loss of mine.<br>I will be deaf to pleading and excuses.<br>Nor [[Tears\|tears]] nor prayers shall purchase out abuses.<br>Therefore use none. Let [[Romeo]] hence in haste,<br>Else, when he is found, that hour is his last.<br>Bear hence this [[Body\|body]], and attend our will.<br>[[Mercy]] but murders, pardoning those that kill. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt with the [[Body\|body]] |

### Atto 3, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Juliet]] |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Gallop apace, you fiery-footed steeds,<br>Towards Phoebus’ lodging. Such a waggoner<br>As Phaeton would whip you to the west<br>And bring in cloudy [[Night\|night]] immediately.<br>Spread thy close curtain, [[Love\|love]]-performing [[Night\|night]],<br>That runaways’ eyes may wink, and [[Romeo]]<br>Leap to these arms untalked of and unseen.<br>[[lovers\|Lovers]] can see to do their amorous rites<br>By their own beauties; or, if [[Love\|love]] be blind,<br>It best agrees with [[Night\|night]]. Come, civil [[Night\|night]],<br>Thou sober-suited matron all in black,<br>And learn me how to lose a winning match<br>Played for a pair of stainless maidenhoods.<br>Hood my unmanned [[Blood\|blood]], bating in my cheeks,<br>With thy black mantle till strange [[Love\|love]] grown bold<br>Think true [[Love\|love]] acted simple modesty.<br>Come [[Night\|night]], come [[Romeo]]; come, thou day in [[Night\|night]],<br>For thou wilt lie upon the wings of [[Night\|night]]<br>Whiter than new [[Snow\|snow]] on a raven’s back.<br>Come, gentle [[Night\|night]]; come, loving, black-browed [[Night\|night]],<br>Give me my [[Romeo]], and when I shall die<br>Take him and cut him out in little [[Stars\|stars]],<br>And he will make the face of heaven so fine<br>That all the world will be in [[Love\|love]] with [[Night\|night]]<br>And pay no worship to the garish sun.<br>O, I have bought the mansion of a [[Love\|love]]<br>But not possessed it, and though I am sold,<br>Not yet enjoyed. So tedious is this day<br>As is the [[Night\|night]] before some festival<br>To an impatient [[Child\|child]] that hath new robes<br>And may not wear them. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Nurse]], wringing her hands, with the ladder of cords in her lap |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | O, here comes my [[Nurse]],<br>And she brings news, and every tongue that speaks<br>But [[Romeo]]’s name speaks heavenly eloquence.<br>Now, [[Nurse]], what news ? What, hast thou there<br>The cords that [[Romeo]] bid thee fetch? |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Nurse\|NURSE]] putting down the cords Ay, ay, the cords. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Ay me, what news? Why dost thou wring thy hands? |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Ah, welladay! He’s dead, he’s dead, he’s dead!<br>We are undone, lady, we are undone.<br>Alack the day, he’s gone, he’s killed, he’s dead! |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Can heaven be so envious? |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | [[Romeo]] can,<br>Though heaven cannot. O [[Romeo]], [[Romeo]],<br>Who ever would have thought it [[Romeo]]? |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | What devil art thou that dost torment me thus?<br>This torture should be roared in dismal hell.<br>Hath [[Romeo]] slain himself? Say thou but ‘Ay’,<br>And that bare vowel ‘I’ shall [[Poison\|poison]] more<br>Than the [[Death\|death]]-darting eye of cockatrice.<br>I am not I if there be such an ‘Ay’,<br>Or those eyes shut that makes thee answer ‘Ay’.<br>If he be slain, say ‘Ay’; or if not, ‘No’.<br>Brief sounds determine of my weal or woe. |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | I saw the wound, I saw it with mine eyes,<br>God save the mark, here on his manly breast—<br>A piteous corpse, a bloody, piteous corpse—<br>Pale, pale as ashes, all bedaubed in [[Blood\|blood]],<br>All in gore [[Blood\|blood]]; I swooned at the sight. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | O, break, my heart, poor bankrupt, break at once!<br>To [[Prison\|prison]], eyes; ne’er look on liberty.<br>Vile earth, to earth resign; end motion here,<br>And thou and [[Romeo]] press one heavy bier! |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | O [[Tybalt]], [[Tybalt]], the best friend I had!<br>O courteous [[Tybalt]], honest gentleman,<br>That ever I should live to see thee dead! |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | What [[Storm\|storm]] is this that blows so contrary?<br>Is [[Romeo]] slaughtered, and is [[Tybalt]] dead?<br>My dearest cousin and my dearer lord?<br>Then, dreadful trumpet, sound the general doom,<br>For who is living if those two are gone? |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | [[Tybalt]] is gone and [[Romeo]] banished.<br>[[Romeo]] that killed him—he is banished. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | O God, did [[Romeo]]’s hand shed [[Tybalt]]’s [[Blood\|blood]]? |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Nurse\|NURSE]] |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | It did, it did, alas the day, it did. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Juliet\|JULIET]] |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | O serpent heart hid with a flow‘ring face!<br>Did ever dragon keep so fair a [[Cave\|cave]]?<br>Beautiful [[Tyrant\|tyrant]], fiend angelical!<br>Dove-feathered raven, wolvish-ravening [[Lamb\|lamb]]!<br>Despisèd substance of divinest show!<br>Just opposite to what thou justly seem’st—<br>A damnèd saint, an honourable [[Villain\|villain]].<br>O [[Nature\|nature]], what hadst thou to do in hell<br>When thou didst bower the spirit of a fiend<br>In mortal paradise of such sweet flesh?<br>Was ever book containing such vile matter<br>So fairly bound? O, that deceit should dwell<br>In such a gorgeous palace! |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | There’s no trust, no [[Faith\|faith]], no honesty in men;<br>All perjured, all forsworn, all naught, dissemblers all.<br>Ah, where’s my man? Give me some aqua vitae.<br>These griefs, these woes, these sorrows make me old.<br>Shame come to [[Romeo]]! |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Blistered be thy tongue<br>For such a wish! He was not born to shame.<br>Upon his brow shame is ashamed to sit,<br>For ’tis a throne where [[Honour\|honour]] may be crowned<br>Sole monarch of the universal earth.<br>O, what a beast was I to chide at him! |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Will you speak [[Well\|well]] of him that killed your cousin? |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Shall I speak ill of him that is my husband?<br>Ah, poor my lord, what tongue shall smooth thy name<br>When I, thy three-hours wife, have mangled it?<br>But wherefore, [[Villain\|villain]], didst thou kill my cousin?<br>That [[Villain\|villain]] cousin would have killed my husband.<br>Back, foolish [[Tears\|tears]], back to your native [[spring]]!<br>Your tributary drops belong to woe,<br>Which you, mistaking, offer up to joy.<br>My husband lives, that [[Tybalt]] would have slain;<br>And [[Tybalt]]’s dead, that would have slain my husband.<br>All this is comfort. Wherefore weep I then?<br>Some word there was, worser than [[Tybalt]]’s [[Death\|death]],<br>That murdered me. I would forget it fain,<br>But O, it presses to my [[Memory\|memory]]<br>Like damned guilty deeds to sinners’ minds!<br>‘[[Tybalt]] is dead, and [[Romeo]] banished.’<br>That ‘banishèd’, that one word ‘banishèd’<br>Hath slain ten thousand Tybalts. [[Tybalt]]’s [[Death\|death]]<br>Was woe enough, if it had ended there;<br>Or, if sour woe delights in fellowship<br>And needly will be ranked with other griefs,<br>Why followed not, when she said ‘[[Tybalt]]’s dead’,<br>‘Thy father’, or ‘thy mother’, nay, or both,<br>Which modern lamentation might have moved?<br>But with a rearward following [[Tybalt]]’s [[Death\|death]],<br>‘[[Romeo]] is banishèd‘-to speak that word<br>Is father, mother, [[Tybalt]], [[Romeo]], [[Juliet]],<br>All slain, all dead. ‘[[Romeo]] is banishèd’—<br>There is no end, no limit, measure, bound,<br>In that word’s [[Death\|death]]. No words can that woe sound.<br>Where is my father and my mother, [[Nurse]]? |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Weeping and wailing over [[Tybalt]]’s corpse.<br>Will you go to them? I will bring you thither. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Wash they his wounds with [[Tears\|tears]]; mine shall be spent<br>When theirs are dry, for [[Romeo]]’s banishment.<br>Take up those cords. Poor ropes, you are beguiled,<br>Both you and I, for [[Romeo]] is exiled.<br>He made you for a highway to my bed,<br>But I, a maid, die maiden-widowed.<br>Come, cords; come, [[Nurse]]; I’ll to my wedding bed,<br>And [[Death\|death]], not [[Romeo]], take my maidenhead! |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | (taking up the cords)<br>Hie to your chamber. I’ll find [[Romeo]]<br>To comfort you. I wot [[Well\|well]] where he is.<br>Hark ye, your [[Romeo]] will be here at [[Night\|night]].<br>I’ll to him. He is hid at Laurence’ cell. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | (giving her a ring)<br>O, find him! Give this ring to my true knight,<br>And bid him come to take his last farewell. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally |

### Atto 3, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Friar Laurence]] |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | [[Romeo]], come forth, come forth, thou [[Fear\|fear]]-full man.<br>Affliction is enamoured of thy parts,<br>And thou art wedded to calamity. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Romeo]] |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | Father, what news? What is the Prince’s doom?<br>What sorrow craves acquaintance at my hand<br>That I yet know not? |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | Too familiar<br>Is my dear son with such sour company.<br>I bring thee tidings of the Prince’s doom. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | What less than doomsday is the Prince’s doom? |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | A gentler judgement vanished from his lips:<br>Not [[Body\|body]]’s [[Death\|death]], but [[Body\|body]]’s banishment. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | Ha, banishment? Be merciful, say ‘[[Death\|death]]’,<br>For [[Exile\|exile]] hath more terror in his look,<br>Much more than [[Death\|death]]. Do not say ‘banishment’. |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | Hence from [[Verona]] art thou banished.<br>Be patient, for the world is broad and wide. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | There is no world without [[Verona]] walls<br>But purgatory, torture, hell itself.<br>Hence banished is banished from the world,<br>And world’s [[Exile\|exile]] is [[Death\|death]]. Then ‘banishèd’<br>Is [[Death\|death]] mistermed. Calling [[Death\|death]] ‘banishèd’<br>Thou cutt‘st my head off with a golden axe,<br>And smil’st upon the stroke that murders me. |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | O deadly sin, O rude unthankfulness!<br>Thy fault our law calls [[Death\|death]], but the kind Prince,<br>Taking thy part, hath rushed aside the law<br>And turned that black word ’[[Death\|death]]’ to banishment.<br>This is dear [[Mercy\|mercy]], and thou seest it not. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | ‘Tis torture, and not [[Mercy\|mercy]]. Heaven is here<br>Where [[Juliet]] lives, and every cat and dog<br>And little mouse, every unworthy thing,<br>Live here in heaven and may look on her,<br>But [[Romeo]] may not. More validity,<br>More honourable state, more courtship lives<br>In carrion flies than [[Romeo]]. They may seize<br>On the white [[Wonder\|wonder]] of dear [[Juliet]]’s hand,<br>And steal immortal blessing from her lips,<br>Who, even in pure and vestal modesty,<br>Still blush, as thinking their own kisses sin.<br>But [[Romeo]] may not, he is banished.<br>Flies may do this, but I from this must fly.<br>They are free men, but I am banished.<br>And sayst thou yet that [[Exile\|exile]] is not [[Death\|death]]?<br>Hadst thou no [[Poison\|poison]] mixed, no sharp-ground knife,<br>No sudden mean of [[Death\|death]], though ne’er so mean,<br>But ‘banishèd’ to kill me—‘banishèd’?<br>O friar, the damned use that word in hell.<br>Howling attends it. How hast thou the heart,<br>Being a divine, a ghostly confessor,<br>A sin-absolver and my friend professed,<br>To mangle me with that word ‘banishèd’? |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | Thou fond mad man, hear me a little speak. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | O, thou wilt speak again of banishment. |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | I’ll give thee armour to keep off that word—<br>Adversity’s sweet milk, [[philosophy]],<br>To comfort thee though thou art banished. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | Yet ‘banishèd’? Hang up [[philosophy]]!<br>Unless [[philosophy]] can make a [[Juliet]],<br>Displant a town, reverse a prince’s doom,<br>It helps not, it prevails not. Talk no more. |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | O, then I see that madmen have no ears. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | How should they, when that wise men have no eyes? |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | Let me dispute with thee of thy estate. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | Thou canst not speak of that thou dost not feel.<br>Wert thou as young as I, [[Juliet]] thy [[Love\|love]],<br>An hour but married, [[Tybalt]] murdered,<br>Doting like me, and like me banished,<br>Then mightst thou speak, then mightst thou tear thy<br>hair,<br>And fall upon the ground, as I do now,<br>He falls upon the ground<br><br>Taking the measure of an unmade [[Grave\|grave]].<br>Knock within |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | Arise, one knocks. Good [[Romeo]], hide thyself. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | Not I, unless the breath of heartsick groans<br>Mist-like enfold me from the search of eyes. |
| *(didascalia)* | Knocking within |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | Hark, how they knock!—Who’s there?—[[Romeo]], arise.<br>Thou wilt be taken.—Stay a white.—Stand up.<br>Still knock within<br>Run to my study.—By and by!—God’s will,<br>What simpleness is this?<br>Knock within<br>I come, I come.<br>Who knocks so hard? Whence come you? What’s your<br>will? |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | (within)<br>Let me come in, and you shall know my errand.<br>I come from Lady [[Juliet]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] opening the door Welcome then. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Nurse]] |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | O holy friar, O tell me, holy friar,<br>Where is my lady’s lord? Where’s [[Romeo]]? |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | There on the ground, with his own [[Tears\|tears]] made drunk. |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | O, he is even in my mistress’ case,<br>Just in her case! O woeful sympathy,<br>Piteous predicament! Even so lies she,<br>Blubb’ring and weeping, weeping and blubb’ring.<br>(To [[Romeo]]) Stand up, stand up, stand an you be a man,<br>For [[Juliet]]’s sake, for her sake, rise and stand.<br>Why should you fall into so deep an O? |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | (rising)<br>[[Nurse]]. |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Ah sir, ah sir, [[Death\|death]]’s the end of all. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | Spak’st thou of [[Juliet]]? How is it with her?<br>Doth not she think me an old murderer,<br>Now I have stained the [[Childhood\|childhood]] of our joy<br>With [[Blood\|blood]] removed but little from her own?<br>Where is she, and how doth she, and what says<br>My concealed lady to our cancelled [[Love\|love]]? |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | O, she says nothing, sir, but weeps and weeps,<br>And now falls on her bed, and then starts up,<br>And ’[[Tybalt]]’ calls, and then on [[Romeo]] cries,<br>And then down falls again. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | As if that name<br>Shot from the deadly level of a gun<br>Did murder her as that name’s cursed hand<br>Murdered her kinsman. O tell me, friar, tell me,<br>In what vile part of this anatomy<br>Doth my name lodge? Tell me, that I may sack<br>The hateful mansion. |
| *(didascalia)* | He offers to stab himself, and the [[Nurse]] snatches the dagger away |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | Hold thy desperate hand.<br>Art thou a man? Thy form cries out thou art.<br>Thy [[Tears\|tears]] are womanish, thy wild acts denote<br>The unreasonable fury of a beast.<br>Unseemly woman in a seeming man,<br>And ill-beseeming beast in seeming both!<br>Thou hast amazed me. By my holy order,<br>I thought thy disposition better tempered.<br>Hast thou slain [[Tybalt]]? Wilt thou slay thyself,<br>And slay thy lady that in thy life lives<br>By doing damned hate upon thyself?<br>Why rail‘st thou on thy birth, the heaven, and earth,<br>Since birth and heaven and earth, all three, do meet<br>In thee at once, which thou at once wouldst lose?<br>Fie, fie, thou sham’st thy shape, thy [[Love\|love]], thy wit,<br>Which like a usurer abound‘st in all,<br>And usest none in that true use indeed<br>Which should bedeck thy shape, thy [[Love\|love]], thy wit.<br>Thy noble shape is but a form of wax,<br>Digressing from the valour of a man;<br>Thy dear [[Love\|love]] sworn but hollow perjury,<br>Killing that [[Love\|love]] which thou hast vowed to cherish;<br>Thy wit, that ornament to shape and [[Love\|love]],<br>Misshapen in the conduct of them both,<br>Like powder in a skilless soldier’s flask<br>Is set afire by thine own ignorance,<br>And thou dismembered with thine own defence.<br>What, rouse thee, man! Thy [[Juliet]] is alive,<br>For whose dear sake thou wast but lately dead:<br>There art thou happy. [[Tybalt]] would kill thee,<br>But thou slewest [[Tybalt]]: there art thou happy.<br>The law that threatened [[Death\|death]] becomes thy friend,<br>And turns it to [[Exile\|exile]]: there art thou happy.<br>A pack of blessings light upon thy back,<br>Happiness courts thee in her best array,<br>But, like a mishavèd and sullen wench,<br>Thou pout’st upon thy fortune and thy [[Love\|love]].<br>Take heed, take heed, for such die miserable.<br>Go, get thee to thy [[Love\|love]], as was decreed.<br>Ascend her chamber; hence and comfort her.<br>But look thou stay not till the watch be set,<br>For then thou canst not pass to Mantua,<br>Where thou shalt live till we can find a [[Time\|time]]<br>To blaze your [[Marriage\|marriage]], reconcile your friends,<br>Beg pardon of the Prince, and call thee back<br>With twenty hundred thousand times more joy<br>Than thou went’st forth in lamentation.<br>Go before, [[Nurse]]. Commend me to thy lady,<br>And bid her hasten all the [[house]] to bed,<br>Which heavy sorrow makes them apt unto.<br>[[Romeo]] is coming. |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | O Lord, I could have stayed here all the [[Night\|night]]<br>To hear good counsel! O, what learning is!<br>My lord, I’ll tell my lady you will come. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | Do so, and bid my sweet prepare to chide. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Nurse]] offers to go in, and turns again |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | (giving the ring)<br>Here, sir, a ring she bid me give you, sir.<br>Hie you, make haste, for it grows very late. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | How [[Well\|well]] my comfort is revived by this. Exit [[Nurse]] |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | Go hence, good [[Night\|night]], and here stands all your state.<br>Either be gone before the watch be set,<br>Or by the break of day disguised from hence.<br>Sojourn in Mantua. I’ll find out your man,<br>And he shall signify from [[Time\|time]] to [[Time\|time]]<br>Every good hap to you that chances here.<br>Give me thy hand. ’Tis late. Farewell. Good [[Night\|night]]. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | But that a joy past joy calls out on me,<br>It were a grief so brief to part with thee. |
| *(didascalia)* | Farewell. Exeunt severally |

### Atto 3, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Capulet]], his Wife, and [[Paris]] |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Things have fall’n out, sir, so unluckily<br>That we have had no [[Time\|time]] to move our daughter.<br>Look you, she loved her kinsman [[Tybalt]] dearly,<br>And so did I. [[Well]], we were born to die.<br>’Tis very late. She’ll not come down tonight.<br>I promise you, but for your company<br>I would have been abed an hour ago. |
| [[Paris\|PARIS]] | These times of woe afford no times to woo.<br>Madam, good [[Night\|night]]. Commend me to your daughter. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | I will, and know her mind early tomorrow.<br>Tonight she’s mewed up to her heaviness. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Paris]] offers to go in, and [[Capulet]] calls him again |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Sir [[Paris]], I will make a desperate tender<br>Of my [[Child\|child]]’s [[Love\|love]]. I think she will be ruled<br>In all respects by me. Nay, more, I [[Doubt\|doubt]] it not.<br>Wife, go you to her ere you go to bed.<br>Acquaint her here of my son [[Paris]]’ [[Love\|love]],<br>And bid her-mark you me?—on Wednesday next—<br>But soft—what day is this? |
| [[Paris\|PARIS]] | Monday, my lord. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Monday. Ha, ha! [[Well]], Wednesday is too soon.<br>O’ [[thursday\|Thursday]] let it be. 0’ [[thursday\|Thursday]], tell her,<br>She shall be married to this noble earl.<br>Will you be ready? Do you like this haste?<br>We’ll keep no great ado—a friend or two.<br>For hark you, [[Tybalt]] being slain so late,<br>It may be thought we held him carelessly,<br>Being our kinsman, if we revel much.<br>Therefore we’ll have some half a dozen friends,<br>And there an end. But what say you to [[thursday\|Thursday]]? |
| [[Paris\|PARIS]] | My lord, I would that [[thursday\|Thursday]] were tomorrow. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | [[Well]], get you gone. O’ [[thursday\|Thursday]] be it, then.<br>(To his Wife) Go you to [[Juliet]] ere you go to bed.<br>Prepare her, wife, against this wedding day.—<br>Farewell, my lord.—Light to my chamber, ho!—<br>Afore me, it is so very late that we<br>May call it early by and by. Good [[Night\|night]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Capulet]] and his wife at one door, [[Paris]] at another door |

### Atto 3, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Romeo]] and [[Juliet]] aloft with the ladder of cords |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Wilt thou be gone? It is not yet near day.<br>It was the [[Nightingale\|nightingale]], and not the lark,<br>That pierced the [[Fear\|fear]]-full hollow of thine ear.<br>Nightly she sings on yon pom’granate tree.<br>Believe me, [[Love\|love]], it was the [[Nightingale\|nightingale]]. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | It was the lark, the herald of the morn,<br>No [[Nightingale\|nightingale]]. Look, [[Love\|love]], what envious streaks<br>Do lace the severing clouds in yonder east.<br>[[Night]]’s candles are burnt out, and jocund day<br>Stands tiptoe on the misty mountain tops.<br>I must be gone and live, or stay and die. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Yon light is not daylight; I know it, I.<br>It is some meteor that the sun exhaled<br>To be to thee this [[Night\|night]] a torchbearer<br>And light thee on thy way to Mantua.<br>Therefore stay yet. Thou need’st not to be gone. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | Let me be ta‘en, let me be put to [[Death\|death]].<br>I am content, so thou wilt have it so.<br>I’ll say yon grey is not the morning’s eye,<br>’Tis but the pale reflex of [[Cynthia]]’s brow;<br>Nor that is not the lark whose notes do beat<br>The vaulty heaven so high above our heads.<br>I have more care to stay than will to go.<br>Come, [[Death\|death]], and welcome; [[Juliet]] wills it so.<br>How is’t, my [[Soul\|soul]]? Let’s talk. It is not day. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | It is, it is. Hie hence, be gone, away.<br>It is the lark that sings so out of tune,<br>Straining harsh discords and unpleasing sharps.<br>Some say the lark makes sweet division;<br>This doth not so, for she divideth us.<br>Some say the lark and loathed toad changed eyes.<br>O, now I would they had changed voices, too,<br>Since arm from arm that voice doth us affray,<br>Hunting thee hence with hunt’s-up to the day.<br>O, now be gone! More light and light it grows. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | More light and light, more dark and dark our woes. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Nurse]] hastily |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Madam. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | [[Nurse]]. |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Your lady mother is coming to your chamber.<br>The day is broke; be wary, look about. Exit |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Then, [[Window\|window]], let day in, and let life out. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | Farewell, farewell! One kiss, and I’ll descend. |
| *(didascalia)* | He lets down the ladder of cords and goes down |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Art thou gone so, [[Love\|love]], lord, my husband, friend?<br>I must hear from thee every day in the hour,<br>For in a minute there are many days.<br>O, by this count I shall be much in years<br>Ere I again behold my [[Romeo]]. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | Farewell.<br>I will omit no opportunity<br>That may convey my greetings, [[Love\|love]], to thee. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | O, think’st thou we shall ever meet again? |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | I [[Doubt\|doubt]] it not, and all these woes shall serve<br>For sweet discourses in our times to come. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Juliet\|JULIET]] |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | O God, I have an ill-divining [[Soul\|soul]]!<br>Methinks I see thee, now thou art so low,<br>As one dead in the [[Bottom\|bottom]] of a tomb.<br>Either my eyesight fails, or thou look’st pale. |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | And trust me, [[Love\|love]], in my eye so do you.<br>Dry sorrow drinks our [[Blood\|blood]]. Adieu, adieu. Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Juliet\|JULIET]] pulling up the ladder and weeping |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | O fortune, fortune, all men call thee fickle.<br>If thou art fickle, what dost thou with him<br>That is renowned for [[Faith\|faith]]? Be fickle, fortune,<br>For then I [[Hope\|hope]] thou wilt not keep him long,<br>But send him back. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Capulet]]’s Wife below |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | Ho, daughter, are you up? |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Who is’t that calls? It is my lady mother.<br>Is she not down so late, or up so early?<br>What unaccustomed cause procures her hither? |
| *(didascalia)* | She goes down and enters below |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | Why, how now, [[Juliet]]? |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Madam, I am not [[Well\|well]]. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | Evermore weeping for your cousin’s [[Death\|death]]?<br>What, wilt thou wash him from his [[Grave\|grave]] with [[Tears\|tears]]?<br>An if thou couldst, thou couldst not make him live,<br>Therefore have done. Some grief shows much of [[Love\|love]],<br>But much of grief shows still some want of wit. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Yet let me weep for such a feeling loss. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | So shall you feel the loss, but not the friend<br>Which you so weep for. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Feeling so the loss,<br>I cannot choose but ever weep the friend. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | [[Well]], girl, thou weep’st not so much for his [[Death\|death]]<br>As that the [[Villain\|villain]] lives which slaughtered him. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | What [[Villain\|villain]], madam? |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | That same [[Villain\|villain]] [[Romeo]]. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | (aside)<br>[[Villain]] and he be many miles asunder.<br>(To her mother) God pardon him—I do, with all my<br>heart,<br>And yet no man like he doth grieve my heart. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | That is because the traitor murderer lives. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Ay, madam, from the reach of these my hands.<br>Would none but I might venge my cousin’s [[Death\|death]]. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | We will have vengeance for it, [[Fear\|fear]] thou not.<br>Then weep no more. I’ll send to one in Mantua,<br>Where that same banished runagate doth live,<br>Shall give him such an unaccustomed dram<br>That he shall soon keep [[Tybalt]] company;<br>And then I [[Hope\|hope]] thou wilt be satisfied. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Indeed, I never shall be satisfied<br>With [[Romeo]] till I behold him, dead,<br>Is my poor heart so for a kinsman vexed.<br>Madam, if you could find out but a man<br>To bear a [[Poison\|poison]], I would temper it<br>That [[Romeo]] should, upon receipt thereof,<br>Soon [[Sleep\|sleep]] in quiet. O, how my heart abhors<br>To hear him named and cannot come to him<br>To wreak the [[Love\|love]] I bore my cousin<br>Upon his [[Body\|body]] that hath slaughtered him! |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | Find thou the means, and I’ll find such a man.<br>But now I’ll tell thee joyful tidings, girl. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | And joy comes [[Well\|well]] in such a needy [[Time\|time]].<br>What are they, I beseech your ladyship? |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | [[Well]], [[Well\|well]], thou hast a careful father, [[Child\|child]];<br>One who, to put thee from thy heaviness,<br>Hath sorted out a sudden day of joy<br>That thou expect’st not, nor I looked not for. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Madam, in happy [[Time\|time]]. What day is that? |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | Marry, my [[Child\|child]], early next [[thursday\|Thursday]] morn<br>The gallant, young, and noble gentleman<br>The County [[Paris]] at Saint Peter’s Church<br>Shall happily make thee there a joyful bride. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Now, by Saint Peter’s Church, and Peter too,<br>He shall not make me there a joyful bride.<br>I [[Wonder\|wonder]] at this haste, that I must wed<br>Ere he that should be husband comes to woo.<br>I pray you, tell my lord and father, madam,<br>I will not marry yet; and when I do, I swear<br>It shall be [[Romeo]]—whom you know I hate—<br>Rather than [[Paris]]. These are news indeed. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Capulet]] and the [[Nurse]] |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | Here comes your father. Tell him so yourself,<br>And see how he will take it at your hands. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | When the sun sets, the earth doth drizzle dew,<br>But for the sunset of my brother’s son<br>It rains downright.<br>How now, a conduit, girl? What, still in [[Tears\|tears]]?<br>Evermore show‘ring? In one little [[Body\|body]]<br>Thou counterfeit’st a barque, a sea, a [[Wind\|wind]],<br>For still thy eyes—which I may call the sea—<br>Do ebb and flow with [[Tears\|tears]]. The barque thy [[Body\|body]] is,<br>Sailing in this salt flood; the winds thy sighs,<br>Who, raging with thy [[Tears\|tears]] and they with them,<br>Without a sudden calm will overset<br>Thy tempest-tossed [[Body\|body]].—How now, wife?<br>Have you delivered to her our decree? |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | Ay, sir, but she will none, she gives you thanks.<br>I would the [[Fool\|fool]] were married to her [[Grave\|grave]]. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Soft, take me with you, take me with you, wife.<br>How, will she none? Doth she not give us thanks?<br>Is she not proud? Doth she not count her blest,<br>Unworthy as she is, that we have wrought<br>So worthy a gentleman to be her bride? |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Not proud you have, but thankful that you have.<br>Proud can I never be of what I hate,<br>But thankful even for hate that is meant [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | How, how, how, how—chopped [[logic]]? What is this?<br>‘Proud’, and ‘I thank you’, and ’I thank you not’,<br>And yet ‘not proud’ ? Mistress minion, you,<br>Thank me no thankings, nor proud me no prouds,<br>But fettle your fine joints ’gainst [[thursday\|Thursday]] next<br>To go with [[Paris]] to Saint Peter’s Church,<br>Or I will drag thee on a hurdle thither.<br>Out, you green-sickness carrion! Out, you baggage,<br>You tallow-face! |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | Fie, fie, what, are you mad? |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | (kneeling)<br>Good father, I beseech you on my knees,<br>Hear me with patience but to speak a word. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Hang thee, young baggage, disobedient wretch!<br>I tell thee what: get thee to church o’ [[thursday\|Thursday]],<br>Or never after look me in the face.<br>Speak not, reply not, do not answer me. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Juliet]] rises |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | My fingers itch. Wife, we scarce thought us blest<br>That God had lent us but this only [[Child\|child]],<br>But now I see this one is one too much,<br>And that we have a curse in having her.<br>Out on her, hilding! |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | God in heaven bless her!<br>You are to blame, my lord, to rate her so. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | And why, my lady Wisdom? Hold your tongue,<br>Good Prudence. Smatter with your gossips, go! |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | I speak no treason. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] O, God-i’-good-e’en! |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Nurse\|NURSE]] |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | May not one speak? |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Peace, you mumbling [[Fool\|fool]],<br>Utter your gravity o’er a gossip’s bowl,<br>For here we need it not. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | You are too hot. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | God’s bread, it makes me mad. Day, [[Night\|night]]; work, play;<br>[[Alone]], in company, still my care hath been<br>To have her matched; and having now provided<br>A gentleman of noble parentage,<br>Of fair demesnes, youthful, and nobly lined,<br>Stuffed, as they say, with honourable parts,<br>Proportioned as one’s thought would wish a man—<br>And then to have a wretched puling [[Fool\|fool]],<br>A whining maumet, in her fortune’s tender,<br>To answer ‘I’ll not wed, I cannot [[Love\|love]];<br>I am too young, I pray you pardon me’!<br>But an you will not wed, I’ll pardon you!<br>Graze where you will, you shall not [[house]] with me.<br>Look to‘t, think on’t. I do not use to jest.<br>[[thursday\|Thursday]] is near. Lay hand on heart. Advise.<br>An you be mine, I’ll give you to my friend.<br>An you be not, hang, beg, starve, die in the streets,<br>For, by my [[Soul\|soul]], I’ll ne’er acknowledge thee,<br>Nor what is mine shall never do thee good.<br>Trust to’t. Bethink you. I’ll not be forsworn. Exit |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Is there no pity sitting in the clouds<br>That sees into the [[Bottom\|bottom]] of my grief’<br>O sweet my mother, cast me not away!<br>Delay this [[Marriage\|marriage]] for a month, a week;<br>Or if you do not, make the bridal bed<br>In that dim monument where [[Tybalt]] lies. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | Talk not to me, for I’ll not speak a word.<br>Do as thou wilt, for I have done with thee. Exit |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | O, God—ONurse, how shall this be prevented?<br>My husband is on earth, my [[Faith\|faith]] in heaven.<br>How shall that [[Faith\|faith]] return again to earth<br>Unless that husband send it me from heaven<br>By leaving earth? Comfort me, counsel me.<br>Alack, alack, that heaven should practise stratagems<br>Upon so soft a subject as myself!<br>What sayst thou? Hast thou not a word of joy?<br>Some comfort, [[Nurse]]. |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | [[Faith]], here it is: [[Romeo]]<br>Is banished, and all the world to nothing<br>That he dares ne‘er come back to challenge you,<br>Or if he do, it needs must be by stealth.<br>Then, since the case so stands as now it doth,<br>I think it best you married with the County.<br>O, he’s a lovely gentleman!<br>[[Romeo]]’s a dishclout to him. An eagle, madam,<br>Hath not so green, so quick, so fair an eye<br>As [[Paris]] hath. Beshrew my very heart,<br>I think you are happy in this second match,<br>For it excels your first; or if it did not,<br>Your first is dead, or ’twere as good he were<br>As living hence and you no use of him. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Speak’st thou from thy heart? |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | And from my [[Soul\|soul]], too, else beshrew them both. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Amen. |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | What? |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | [[Well]], thou hast comforted me marvellous much.<br>Go in; and tell my lady I am gone,<br>Having displeased my father, to Laurence’ cell<br>To make confession and to be absolved. |
| *(didascalia)* | Marry, I will; and this is wisely done. Exit |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | (watching her go)<br>Ancient damnation! O most wicked fiend!<br>Is it more sin to wish me thus forsworn,<br>Or to dispraise my lord with that same tongue<br>Which she hath praised him with above compare<br>So many thousand times? Go, counsellor!<br>Thou and my bosom henceforth shall be twain.<br>I’ll to the friar, to know his remedy.<br>If all else fail, myself have power to die. Exit |

### Atto 4, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Friar Laurence]] and [[Paris]] |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | On [[thursday\|Thursday]], sir? The [[Time\|time]] is very short. |
| [[Paris\|PARIS]] | My father [[Capulet]] will have it so,<br>And I am nothing slow to slack his haste. |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | You say you do not know the lady’s mind?<br>Uneven is the course. I like it not. |
| [[Paris\|PARIS]] | Immoderately she weeps for [[Tybalt]]’s [[Death\|death]],<br>And therefore have I little talked of [[Love\|love]],<br>For [[Venus]] smiles not in a [[house]] of [[Tears\|tears]].<br>Now, sir, her father counts it dangerous<br>That she do give her sorrow so much sway,<br>And in his wisdom hastes our [[Marriage\|marriage]]<br>To stop the inundation of her [[Tears\|tears]],<br>Which, too much minded by herself [[Alone\|alone]],<br>May be put from her by society.<br>Now do you know the reason of this haste. |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | (aside)<br>I would I knew not why it should be slowed.— |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Juliet]] |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | Look, sir, here comes the lady toward my cell. |
| [[Paris\|PARIS]] | Happily met, my lady and my wife. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | That may be, sir, when I may be a wife. |
| [[Paris\|PARIS]] | That ’may be’ must be, [[Love\|love]], on [[thursday\|Thursday]] next. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | What must be shall be. |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | That’s a certain text. |
| [[Paris\|PARIS]] | Come you to make confession to this father? |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | To answer that, I should confess to you. |
| [[Paris\|PARIS]] | Do not deny to him that you [[Love\|love]] me. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | I will confess to you that I [[Love\|love]] him. |
| [[Paris\|PARIS]] | So will ye, I am sure, that you [[Love\|love]] me. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | If I do so, it will be of more price,<br>Being spoke behind your back, than to your face. |
| [[Paris\|PARIS]] | Poor [[Soul\|soul]], thy face is much abused with [[Tears\|tears]]. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | The [[Tears\|tears]] have got small victory by that,<br>For it was bad enough before their spite. |
| [[Paris\|PARIS]] | Thou wrong’st it more than [[Tears\|tears]] with that report. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | That is no slander, sir, which is a [[Truth\|truth]],<br>And what I spake, I spake it to my face. |
| [[Paris\|PARIS]] | Thy face is mine, and thou hast slandered it. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | It may be so, for it is not mine own.—<br>Are you at leisure, holy father, now,<br>Or shall I come to you at evening mass? |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | My leisure serves me, pensive daughter, now.<br>My lord, we must entreat the [[Time\|time]] [[Alone\|alone]]. |
| [[Paris\|PARIS]] | God shield I should disturb devotion!—<br>[[Juliet]], on [[thursday\|Thursday]] early will I rouse ye.<br>(Kissing her) Till then, adieu, and keep this holy kiss. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | O, shut the door, and when thou hast done so,<br>Come weep with me, past [[Hope\|hope]], past cure, past help! |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | O [[Juliet]], I already know thy grief.<br>It strains me past the compass of my wits.<br>I hear thou must, and nothing may prorogue it,<br>On [[thursday\|Thursday]] next be married to this County. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Tell me not, friar, that thou hear’st of this,<br>Unless thou tell me how I may prevent it.<br>If in thy wisdom thou canst give no help,<br>Do thou but call my resolution wise,<br>She draws a knife<br>And with this knife I’ll help it presently.<br>God joined my heart and [[Romeo]]‘s, thou our hands,<br>And ere this hand, by thee to [[Romeo]]’s sealed,<br>Shall be the label to another deed,<br>Or my true heart with treacherous revolt<br>Turn to another, this shall slay them both.<br>Therefore, out of thy long-experienced [[Time\|time]],<br>Give me some present counsel; or, behold,<br>’Twixt my extremes and me this bloody knife<br>Shall play the umpire, arbitrating that<br>Which the commission of thy years and art<br>Could to no issue of true [[Honour\|honour]] bring.<br>Be not so long to speak. I long to die<br>If what thou speak’st speak not of remedy. |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | Hold, daughter, I do spy a kind of [[Hope\|hope]]<br>Which craves as desperate an execution<br>As that is desperate which we would prevent.<br>If, rather than to marry County [[Paris]],<br>Thou hast the strength of will to slay thyself,<br>Then is it likely thou wilt undertake<br>A thing like [[Death\|death]] to chide away this shame,<br>That cop‘st with [[Death\|death]] himself to scape from it;<br>And, if thou dar’st, I’ll give thee remedy. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | O, bid me leap, rather than marry [[Paris]],<br>From off the battlements of any tower,<br>Or walk in thievish ways, or bid me lurk<br>Where serpents are. Chain me with roaring bears,<br>Or hide me nightly in a charnel [[house]],<br>O’ercovered quite with dead men’s rattling bones,<br>With reeky shanks and yellow chapless skulls;<br>Or bid me go into a new-made [[Grave\|grave]]<br>And hide me with a dead man in his tomb—<br>Things that, to hear them told, have made me<br>tremble—<br>And I will do it without [[Fear\|fear]] or [[Doubt\|doubt]],<br>To live an unstained wife to my sweet [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | Hold, then; go [[Home\|home]], be merry, give consent<br>To marry [[Paris]]. Wednesday is tomorrow.<br>Tomorrow [[Night\|night]] look that thou lie [[Alone\|alone]].<br>Let not the [[Nurse]] lie with thee in thy chamber.<br>Take thou this vial, being then in bed,<br>And this distilling liquor drink thou off,<br>When presently through all thy veins shall run<br>A cold and drowsy [[Humour\|humour]]; for no pulse<br>Shall keep his native progress, but surcease.<br>No warmth, no breath shall testify thou livest.<br>The roses in thy lips and cheeks shall fade<br>To wanny ashes, thy eyes’ windows fall<br>Like [[Death\|death]] when he shuts up the day of life.<br>Each part, deprived of supple [[government]],<br>Shall, stiff and stark and cold, appear like [[Death\|death]];<br>And in this borrowed likeness of shrunk [[Death\|death]]<br>Thou shalt continue two-and-forty hours,<br>And then awake as from a pleasant [[Sleep\|sleep]].<br>Now, when the bridegroom in the morning comes<br>To rouse thee from thy bed, there art thou dead.<br>Then, as the manner of our country is,<br>In thy best robes, uncovered on the bier<br>Thou shalt be borne to that same ancient vault<br>Where all the kindred of the Capulets lie.<br>In the meantime, against thou shalt awake,<br>Shall [[Romeo]] by my letters know our drift,<br>And hither shall he come, and he and I<br>Will watch thy waking, and that very [[Night\|night]]<br>Shall [[Romeo]] bear thee hence to Mantua.<br>And this shall free thee from this present shame,<br>If no inconstant toy nor womanish [[Fear\|fear]]<br>Abate thy valour in the acting it. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Give me, give me O, tell not me of [[Fear\|fear]]! |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | (giving her the vial)<br>Hold, get you gone. Be strong and prosperous<br>In this resolve. I’ll send a friar with [[Speed\|speed]]<br>To Mantua with my letters to thy lord. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | [[Love]] give me strength, and strength shall help afford.<br>Farewell, dear father. Exeunt [severally] |

### Atto 4, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Capulet]], his Wife, the [[Nurse]], and two Servingmen |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | (giving a Servingman a paper)<br>So many guests invite as here are writ. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Servingman |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | (To the other Servingman) Sirrah, go hire me twenty<br>cunning cooks. |
| SERVINGMAN | You shall have none ill, sir, for I’ll try if they can lick their fingers. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | How canst thou try them so? |
| SERVINGMAN | Marry, sir, ’tis an ill cook that cannot lick his own fingers, therefore he that cannot lick his fingers goes not with me. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] Go, be gone. Exit Servingman |
| SERVINGMAN | We shall be much unfurnished for this [[Time\|time]].<br>(To the [[Nurse]]) What, is my daughter gone to Friar<br>Laurence? |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Ay, forsooth. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | [[Well]], he may chance to do some good on her.<br>A peevish, [[Self\|self]]-willed harlotry it is. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Juliet]] |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | See where she comes from shrift with merry look. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | (to [[Juliet]])<br>How now, my headstrong, where have you been gadding ? |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Where I have learned me to repent the sin<br>Of disobedient opposition<br>To you and your behests, and am enjoined<br>By holy Laurence to fall prostrate here<br>To beg your pardon. (Kneeling) Pardon, I beseech you.<br>Henceforward I am ever ruled by you. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] to the [[Nurse]] |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Send for the County; go tell him of this.<br>I’ll have this knot knit up tomorrow morning. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | I met the youthful lord at Laurence’ cell,<br>And gave him what becoming [[Love\|love]] I might,<br>Not stepping o’er the bounds of modesty. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Why, I am glad on’t. This is [[Well\|well]]. Stand up.<br>[[Juliet]] rises<br>This is as’t should be. Let me see the County. |
| *(didascalia)* | To [[Nurse]] Ay, marry, go, I say, and fetch him hither. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Now, afore God, this reverend holy friar,<br>All our whole [[City\|city]] is much bound to him. |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | [[Nurse]], will you go with me into my closet<br>To help me sort such needful ornaments<br>As you think fit to furnish me tomorrow? |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | No, not till [[thursday\|Thursday]]. There is [[Time\|time]] enough. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Go, [[Nurse]], go with her. We’ll to church tomorrow. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Juliet]] and [[Nurse]] |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | We shall be short in our provision.<br>’Tis now near [[Night\|night]]. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Tush, I will stir about,<br>And all things shall be [[Well\|well]], I warrant thee, wife.<br>Go thou to [[Juliet]], help to deck up her.<br>I’ll not to bed tonight. Let me [[Alone\|alone]].<br>I’ll play the housewife for this once. What, ho!<br>They are all forth. [[Well]], I will walk myself<br>To County [[Paris]] to prepare up him<br>Against tomorrow. My heart is wondrous light,<br>Since this same wayward girl is so reclaimed. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally |

### Atto 4, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Juliet]] and the [[Nurse]] with garments |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Ay, those attires are best. But, gentle [[Nurse]],<br>I pray thee leave me to myself tonight,<br>For I have need of many orisons<br>To move the heavens to smile upon my state,<br>Which—[[Well\|well]] thou knowest—is [[Cross\|cross]] and full of sin. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Capulet]]’s Wife |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | What, are you busy, ho? Need you my help? |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | No, madam, we have culled such necessaries<br>As are behoveful for our state tomorrow.<br>So please you, let me now be left [[Alone\|alone]],<br>And let the [[Nurse]] this [[Night\|night]] sit up with you,<br>For I am sure you have your hands full all<br>In this so sudden business. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | Good [[Night\|night]].<br>Get thee to bed, and rest, for thou hast need. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Capulet]]’s Wife and [[Nurse]] |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | Farewell. God knows when we shall meet again.<br>I have a faint cold [[Fear\|fear]] thrills through my veins<br>That almost freezes up the heat of life.<br>I’ll call them back again to comfort me.<br>[[Nurse]]!—What should she do here? |
| *(didascalia)* | She opens curtains, behind which is seen her bed |
| [[Juliet\|JULIET]] | My dismal scene I needs must act [[Alone\|alone]].<br>Come, vial. What if this mixture do not work at all?<br>Shall I be married then tomorrow morning?<br>No, no, this shall forbid it. Lie thou there.<br>She lays down a knife<br>What if it be a [[Poison\|poison]] which the friar<br>Subtly hath ministered to have me dead,<br>Lest in this [[Marriage\|marriage]] he should be dishonoured<br>Because he married me before to [[Romeo]]?<br>I [[Fear\|fear]] it is—and yet methinks it should not,<br>For he hath still been tried a holy man.<br>How if, when I am laid into the tomb,<br>I wake before the [[Time\|time]] that [[Romeo]]<br>Come to redeem me? There’s a fearful point.<br>Shall I not then be stifled in the vault,<br>To whose foul mouth no healthsome air breathes in,<br>And there die strangled ere my [[Romeo]] comes?<br>Or, if I live, is it not very like<br>The horrible conceit of [[Death\|death]] and [[Night\|night]],<br>Together with the terror of the place—<br>As in a vault, an ancient receptacle<br>Where for this many hundred years the bones<br>Of all my buried ancestors are packed;<br>Where bloody [[Tybalt]], yet but green in earth,<br>Lies fest‘ring in his shroud; where, as they say,<br>At some hours in the [[Night\|night]] spirits resort—<br>Alack, alack, is it not like that I,<br>So early waking—what with loathsome smells,<br>And shrieks like mandrakes torn out of the earth,<br>That living mortals, hearing them, run mad—<br>O, if I wake, shall I not be distraught,<br>Environèd with all these hideous fears,<br>And madly play with my forefathers’ joints,<br>And pluck the mangled [[Tybalt]] from his shroud,<br>And, in this rage, with some great kinsman’s bone<br>As with a club dash out my desp’rate brains?<br>O, look! Methinks I see my cousin’s [[Ghost\|ghost]]<br>Seeking out [[Romeo]] that did spit his [[Body\|body]]<br>Upon a rapier’s point. Stay, [[Tybalt]], stay!<br>[[Romeo]], [[Romeo]], [[Romeo]]! Here’s drink. I drink to thee. |
| *(didascalia)* | She drinks from the vial and falls upon the bed, pulling closed the curtains |

### Atto 4, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Capulet]]’s Wife, and the [[Nurse]] With herbs |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | Hold, take these keys, and fetch more spices, [[Nurse]]. |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | They call for dates and quinces in the pastry. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Capulet]] |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Come, stir, stir, stir! The second cock hath crowed.<br>The curfew bell hath rung. ’Tis three o’[[Clock\|clock]].<br>Look to the baked meats, good Angelica.<br>Spare not for cost. |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Go, you cot-quean, go.<br>Get you to bed. [[Faith]], you’ll be sick tomorrow<br>For this [[Night\|night]]’s watching. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | No, not a whit. What, I have watched ere now<br>All [[Night\|night]] for lesser cause, and ne’er been sick. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | Ay, you have been a mouse-hunt in your [[Time\|time]],<br>But I will watch you from such watching now. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Capulet]]’s Wife and [[Nurse]] |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | A jealous-hood, a jealous-hood! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter three or four Servingmen, with spits and logs and baskets |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Now, fellow, what is there? |
| FIRST SERVINGMAN | Things for the cook, sir, but I know not what. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Make haste, make haste. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit First Servingman and one or two others |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Sirrah, fetch drier logs.<br>Call Peter. He will show thee where they are. |
| SECOND SERVINGMAN | I have a head, sir, that will find out logs<br>And never trouble Peter for the matter. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Mass, and [[Well\|well]] said! A merry whoreson, ha!<br>Thou shalt be loggerhead. Exit Second Servingman<br><br>Good [[Faith\|faith]], ’tis day.<br>The County will be here with music straight,<br>For so he said he would. |
| *(didascalia)* | Music plays within |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | I hear him near.<br>[[Nurse]]! Wife! What ho, what, [[Nurse]], I say! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Nurse]] |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Go waken [[Juliet]]. Go and trim her up.<br>I’ll go and chat with [[Paris]]. Hie, make haste,<br>Make haste, the bridegroom he is come already.<br>Make haste, I say. Exit |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Mistress, what, mistress! [[Juliet]]! Fast, I warrant her, she.<br>Why, [[Lamb\|lamb]], why, lady! Fie, you slug-abed!<br>Why, [[Love\|love]], I say, madam, sweetheart, why, bride!<br>What, not a word? You take your pennyworths now.<br>[[Sleep]] for a week, for the next [[Night\|night]], I warrant,<br>The County [[Paris]] hath set up his rest<br>That you shall rest but little. God forgive me!<br>Marry, and amen. How sound is she asleep!<br>I needs must wake her. Madam, madam, madam!<br>Ay, let the County take you in your bed.<br>He’ll fright you up, i’[[Faith\|faith]]. Will it not be? |
| *(didascalia)* | She draws back the curtains |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | What, dressed and in your clothes, and down again?<br>I must needs wake you. Lady, lady, lady!<br>Alas, alas! Help, help! My lady’s dead.<br>O welladay, that ever I was born!<br>Some aqua-vitae, ho! My lord, my lady! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Capulet]]’s Wife |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | What noise is here? |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | O lamentable day! |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | What is the matter? |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Look, look. O heavy day! |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | O me, O me, my [[Child\|child]], my only life!<br>Revive, look up, or I will die with thee.<br>Help, help, call help! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Capulet]] |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | For shame, bring [[Juliet]] forth. Her lord is come. |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | She’s dead, deceased. She’s dead, alack the day! |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | Alack the day, she’s dead, she’s dead, she’s dead! |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Ha, let me see her! Out, alas, she’s cold.<br>Her [[Blood\|blood]] is settled, and her joints are stiff.<br>Life and these lips have long been separated.<br>[[Death]] lies on her like an untimely [[Frost\|frost]]<br>Upon the sweetest flower of all the field. |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | O lamentable day! |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | O woeful [[Time\|time]]! |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | [[Death]], that hath ta’en her hence to make me wail,<br>Ties up my tongue, and will not let me speak. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Friar Laurence]] and [[Paris]], with Musicians |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | Come, is the bride ready to go to church? |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Ready to go, but never to return.<br>(To [[Paris]]) O son, the [[Night\|night]] before thy wedding day<br>Hath [[Death\|death]] lain with thy wife. See, there she lies,<br>Flower as she was, deflowered by him.<br>[[Death]] is my son-in-law, [[Death\|death]] is my heir.<br>My daughter he hath wedded. I will die,<br>And leave him all. Life, living, all is [[Death\|death]]’s. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Paris]], [[Capulet]] and his Wife, and the [[Nurse]] all at once wring their hands and cry out together: |
| [[Paris\|PARIS]] | Have I thought long to see this morning’s face,<br>And doth it give me such a sight as this?<br>Beguiled, divorced, wronged, spited, slain!<br>Most detestable [[Death\|death]], by thee beguiled,<br>By cruel, cruel thee quite overthrown.<br>O [[Love\|love]], O life: not life, but [[Love\|love]] in [[Death\|death]]. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]]’S WIFE | Accursed, unhappy, wretched, hateful day!<br>Most miserable hour that e’er [[Time\|time]] saw<br>In lasting labour of his pilgrimage!<br>But one, poor one, one poor and loving [[Child\|child]],<br>But one thing to rejoice and solace in,<br>And cruel [[Death\|death]] hath catched it from my sight! |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | O woe! O woeful, woeful, woeful day!<br>Most lamentable day! Most woeful day<br>That ever, ever, I did yet behold!<br>O day, O day, O day, O hateful day,<br>Never was seen so black a day as this I<br>O woeful day, O woeful day! 85 |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | Despised, distressed, hated, martyred, killed!<br>Uncomfortable [[Time\|time]], why cam’st thou now<br>To murder, murder our solemnity?<br>O [[Child\|child]], O [[Child\|child]], my [[Soul\|soul]] and not my [[Child\|child]]!<br>Dead art thou, alack, my [[Child\|child]] is dead,<br>And with my [[Child\|child]] my joys are buried. |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | Peace, ho, for shame! Confusion’s cure lives not<br>In these confusions. Heaven and yourself<br>Had part in this fair maid. Now heaven hath all,<br>And all the better is it for the maid.<br>Your part in her you could not keep from [[Death\|death]],<br>But heaven keeps his part in eternal life.<br>The most you sought was her promotion,<br>For ’twas your heaven she should be advanced,<br>And weep ye now, seeing she is advanced<br>Above the clouds as high as heaven itself?<br>O, in this [[Love\|love]] you [[Love\|love]] your [[Child\|child]] so ill<br>That you run mad, seeing that she is [[Well\|well]].<br>She’s not [[Well\|well]] married that lives married long,<br>But she’s best married that dies married young.<br>Dry up your [[Tears\|tears]], and stick your rosemary<br>On this fair corpse, and, as the custom is,<br>All in her best array bear her to church;<br>For though fond [[Nature\|nature]] bids us all lament,<br>Yet [[Nature\|nature]]’s [[Tears\|tears]] are reason’s merriment. |
| [[Capulet\|CAPULET]] | All things that we ordained festival<br>Turn from their office to black funeral.<br>Our instruments to [[Melancholy\|melancholy]] [[Bells\|bells]],<br>Our wedding cheer to a sad burial feast,<br>Our solemn hymns to sullen dirges change;<br>Our bridal [[Flowers\|flowers]] serve for a buried corpse,<br>And all things change them to the contrary. |
| [[Friar Laurence\|FRIAR LAURENCE]] | Sir, go you in; and madam, go with him,<br>And go, Sir [[Paris]]. Everyone prepare<br>To follow this fair corpse unto her [[Grave\|grave]].<br>The heavens do lour upon you for some ill.<br>Move them no more by crossing their high will. |
| *(didascalia)* | They cast rosemary on [[Juliet]], and shut the curtains. Exeunt all but the [[Nurse]] and Musicians |
| *(didascalia)* | FIRST MUSICIAN [[Faith]], we may put up our pipes and be gone. |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Honest good fellows, ah, put up, put up,<br>For [[Well\|well]] you know this is a pitiful case. |
| *(didascalia)* | FIRST MUSICIAN |
| [[Nurse\|NURSE]] | Ay, by my troth, the case may be amended. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[Nurse]] Enter Peter |
| PETER | Musicians, O, musicians! ‘Heart’s ease’, ‘Heart’s ease’; O,an you will have me live, play ‘Heart’s ease’. |
| *(didascalia)* | FIRST MUSICIAN Why ‘Heart’s ease’? |
| PETER | O, musicians, because my heart itself plays ‘My heart is full of woe’. O, play me some merry dump to comfort me. |
| *(didascalia)* | FIRST MUSICIAN Not a dump, we. ’Tis no [[Time\|time]] to play now. |
| PETER | You will not then? |
| FIRST MUSICIAN | No. |
| PETER | I will then give it you soundly. |
| FIRST MUSICIAN | What will you give us? |
| PETER | No money, on my [[Faith\|faith]], but the gleek. I will give you the minstrel. |
| FIRST MUSICIAN | Then will I give you the serving-creature. |
| PETER | (drawing his dagger) Then will I lay the serving-creature’s dagger on your pate. I will carry no crochets. I’ll re you, I’ll fa you. Do you note me? |
| FIRST MUSICIAN | An you re us and fa us, you note us. |
| SECOND MUSICIAN | Pray you, put up your dagger and put out your wit. |
| *(didascalia)* | PETER Then have at you with my wit. I will dry-beat you with an iron wit, and put up my iron dagger. Answer me like men. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sings |
| SECOND MUSICIAN | When griping grief the heart doth wound,<br>And doleful dumps the mind oppress,<br>Then music with her silver sound—<br>Why ‘silver sound’, why ‘music with her silver sound’?<br>What say you, Matthew Minikin? |
| FIRST MUSICIAN | Marry, sir, because silver hath a sweet sound. |
| PETER | Prates! What say you, Hugh Rebec? |
| SECOND MUSICIAN | I say ’silver sound’ because musicians sound for silver. |
| PETER | Prates too! What say you, Simon Soundpost? |
| THIRD MUSICIAN | [[Faith]], I know not what to say. |
| PETER | O, I cry you [[Mercy\|mercy]], you are the singer. I will say for you. It is ’music with her silver sound’ because musicians have no gold for sounding. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sings |
| PETER | Then music with her silver sound<br>With speedy help doth lend redress. Exit |
| FIRST MUSICIAN | What a pestilent knave is this same! |
| SECOND MUSICIAN | Hang him, jack! Come, we’ll in here, tarry for the mourners, and stay dinner. Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Romeo]] |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | If I may trust the flattering [[Truth\|truth]] of [[Sleep\|sleep]],<br>My [[Dreams\|dreams]] presage some joyful news at hand.<br>My bosom’s lord sits lightly in his throne,<br>And all this day an unaccustomed spirit<br>Lifts me above the ground with cheerful thoughts.<br>I dreamt my lady came and found me dead—<br>Strange dream, that gives a dead man leave to<br>think!—<br>And breathed such life with kisses in my lips<br>That I revived and was an emperor.<br>Ah me, how sweet is [[Love\|love]] itself possessed<br>When but [[Love\|love]]’s shadows are so rich in joy! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Balthasar, [[Romeo]]’s man, booted |
| [[Romeo\|ROMEO]] | News from [[Verona]]! How now, Balthasar?<br>Dost thou not bring me letters from the friar?<br>How doth my lady? Is my father [[Well\|well]]?<br>How fares my [[Juliet]]? That I ask again,<br>For nothing can be ill if she be [[Well\|well]]. |
